ParlaMint-DK_20170524130121	Thank you, President. Users of Copenhagen harbour, canal, roundabout boats, rowing and kayaking clubs and private boat owners have for many years contacted City & Port in the City of Copenhagen and the Port Police to report the many water scooters that sail through ports and canals at extremely high speeds to great danger for those who are legally in the port.What will the Minister do to ensure that the rules are complied with?
ParlaMint-DK_20170524130147	The minister.
ParlaMint-DK_20170524130149	Thank you and thank you for the question. In recent years the harbour areas have become part of the city's space, and this also means, of course, that the police have experienced an increasing need for port patrols. In particular, speedboats and water scooters are an increasing problem. Unfortunately, we saw earlier this month what tragic consequences it can have when you sail wildly and crazy on water scooter. As a result of the increasing use of the port for recreational purposes, the Copenhagen Police and other police circles have decided to intensify their efforts in the port areas and be more visible in the port. The Copenhagen Police were therefore also present at the port on the day of the accident. Unfortunately, it didn't stop the accident. One of the challenges facing police action is that it is very difficult in the situation to apprehend those who violate the rules on water. On a water scooter one can quickly get out into the open sea, where it is extremely difficult to stop a vessel trying to escape. I come from a meeting with the Mayor of Copenhagen, Frank Jensen, the management at City & Port and the police, where we discussed what action is needed. So we do what we can to avoid any more terrible accidents. One of my suggestions is to give the police better tools to identify those responsible by requiring that water scooters be held responsible and be identified by means of a clear number, a kind of license plate for water scooters, if you like. This will give the police a significantly improved opportunity to identify water scooters and subsequently punish them for their offences. At the same time, we will look at whether there is a need to punish the offenders more severely, for example. by confiscating and confiscating water scooters from drivers who have repeatedly or insanely sailed against the rules. In addition, we are also looking at how we can set up the port areas, for example. by setting up video surveillance to improve port security. These are some of the proposals we are working on. In the near future, we will continue to work and involve relevant actors.

As the rules are today, violations of the Personal Data Act are punishable by fines or imprisonment for up to 4 months. In relation to the specific case, the employees affected by this have the opportunity to complain to the Data Protection Agency. The Data Protection Agency also has the opportunity to deal with the matter of its own motion. As the Data Protection Agency is an independent supervisory authority, I do not have the opportunity, as a minister, to tell the Data Protection Agency to take the matter up, and therefore I cannot say anything today about the consequences of the matter, but I do think that this case shows that personal data protection is being taken seriously. With the new data protection regulation, which will take effect in approx. One year, there is a prospect of further tightening up the level of sanctions in fact, a sharp increase in the level of penalties for breaches of personal data law. It will be significantly higher fines for the companies that come and, moreover, a company that has inadvertently had a security breach will be obliged to notify the breach to the Data Protection Agency. Just to conclude, I have a clear expectation that, as a company, you will behave responsibly when you process personal data about your employees. And if you don't, yes, it'll have consequences.

ParlaMint-DK_20170524132922	The minister.
ParlaMint-DK_20170524132923	First of all, I would like to say that it was a terrible tragic accident that happened in the harbour, where these two girls died unnecessarily. The task of keeping law and order is, of course, a police task, and that is whether it is on land or at sea, and therefore also, of course, when it comes to ports. The most important principle is that the solution of police tasks must be carried out by police staff and with equipment at the disposal of the police. In particular situations, the police may be assisted in the task-solving of other authorities, for example: the defence, including the National Guard. The special situations are where it is either an appropriate consequence of the operation of the military defence, otherwise, or where civil society's resources in the field in question are not sufficient and are not as suitable as the defence resources. In cases where the police may wish to take action at sea against, for example, water scooters sailing crazy, the defense and the Home Guard have the power of vessels suitable to assist the police. And I am glad, as Minister for Defence, that the defence and the Home Guard can help the police when it is needed, and it is my impression that the desire for assistance is met and that cooperation between the authorities works well. ParlaMint-DK_20170524141403	Does the Minister agree that 64-year-old seniors in work that has proved to have worked full time in the years up to 2016 and is therefore entitled to a tax reduction of potentially DKK 100,000, but who will not receive this reduction as a result of an imprecise way of calculating working hours at ATP, should be given a new opportunity to correct ATP's information and thus receive the bonus they are offered in view of the former VK government?
ParlaMint-DK_20170524141411	The minister.
ParlaMint-DK_20170524141430	Thank you for the question, which is the last question to me in this assembly. The senior rejection scheme, which is the subject of the question, was for a narrow group of people, namely full-time workers from some specific vintages. And the targeting was demonstrated by the fact that, when the scheme was introduced back in 2008, rather strict conditions were laid down for the application of the scheme. It is unfortunate and unfortunate that a system of such good purpose in this way ends up disappointing some seniors. Mr. Jesper Petersen to the calculation of working time and the requirement of full-time employment. The calculation of the employment requirement is made by ATP on the basis of the ATP contribution reported by the employer. ATP sends the information to the relevant persons who then have 3 months to object. This also applies if the employer has provided incorrect information to ATP. So there is an opportunity to correct any incorrect information if you turn to ATP within 3 months. After all, ATP does not belong to my area of responsibility, but I would like to say that the Minister for Employment, with whom I have close contact and dialogue, and with which I consider to be a good colleague, and I have both understood that ATP also has a late objection, that is. after 3 months, will resume the determination of an employment rate if there are particularly excusable circumstances or regulatory errors. I do not know in which cases the questioner thinks that the ATP, as formulated in the question, should have an imprecise way of calculating working hours, but these must be cases where the person concerned has not been aware of the 3-month period in force and where ATP's inventory cannot be calculated as a regulatory error.

ParlaMint-DK_20170524144131	The minister.
ParlaMint-DK_20170524144133	Thank you very much for the question. Danish Church in Sydslesvig plays a very central role for the Danish minority in Sydslesvig. Fortunately, there are many of us who are interested in this and attach great importance to the historically close ties between Denmark and the Danish minority. Both the questioner and I, together with two former church ministers, had the opportunity to experience this just four weeks ago at the church day in South Schleswig, where there was a great deal of sympathy for the desire to influence the election of the bishops when it had to take place. It is, in fact, a sympathy which I share and which I also think the questioner shares, but the question is not quite as simple to answer as it might seem. This isn't the first time we've had this question. Danish sailors' and foreign churches, what is called DSUK, employ the priests in South Schleswig, and according to their own statutes they are a private church organisation that is affiliated to the church. So every year DSUK receives a grant from the Finance Act for 22 priestly positions in South Schleswig, and this reflects the special status of the Danish Church in South Schleswig for the Danes who live south of the border for historical reasons. Since 2010, the priests of South Schleswig have been recognised as priests in the church, and therefore they are under the ecclesiastical supervision of a bishop currently in Haderslev. But neither the bishop nor the minister of the church has the right of appointment to the priests. So the democratic tradition of the Danish National Church, in which church councils and priests choose who is to be bishop in their rigidity, is precisely linked to the bishop's special powers towards the established priests and parishioners. In Denmark, therefore, the bishop has both an ecclesiastical and a general legal oversight of the established priests. The bishop also oversees the functions of the congregational councils and, through the power of the institution, supervises the financial management of the congregational councils. The bishop today, as the situation is, does not have the corresponding powers in relation to priests and congregations under the Danish church in Sydslesvig. So the situation is a little different down there. At first sight, I believe that voting rights in the bishop's election must be combined if we are to implement this, but the discussion on whether or not we are interested in some of the supervisory obligations that I have just mentioned in the Danish Church in South Schleswig, and the discussion on DSUK, we still have the benefit. So this is not something you can just decide to do, because so many questions are attached to it. I therefore believe that what we can do in the first place, and I would like to take the initiative in this respect, is to have a discussion of both the wishes and needs that may exist, but also a discussion of the scope of both rights and obligations. I think we should take the initiative on this. ParlaMint-DK_20170524144824	Here's the questioner for reading.
ParlaMint-DK_20170524144826	Can the Minister explain the government's position on current conditions and forward-looking initiatives for timelyness and speed on the coastal track, and does the Minister agree that one should be even more ambitious for the timeliness and speed of the coastline, which is Denmark's most used route?
ParlaMint-DK_20170524144850	The minister.
ParlaMint-DK_20170524144851	The coastal railway is not entirely Denmark's most used route, but it is a very important route. In February, in another reply, I was able to point out a number of reasons for the problems of punctuality on the coast coast. Compared with that time, the Swedish Government, with effect from 4 May, has waived the requirement for identity checks on passengers entering Sweden. Trains will no longer have to make a long stay at Copenhagen Airport. This will create a better flow for the trains towards Kastrup, and it will also be felt on the coastal track. Border controls themselves have not been abolished, but they are carried out in Sweden. In addition, we are currently discussing the possibilities for practical and organisational simplification of traffic across the Sound with the Swedish traffic buyer Scania traffic. The principle of the discussions is to divide current Øresund traffic into the stretch between Malmø and Østerport and between Copenhagen and Elsinore. Sweden will be responsible for the Øresund traffic to and from Copenhagen, while the Coastal Railway between Copenhagen and Elsinore will be a purely Danish matter. This will allow the coastal railway to be decoupled from Swedish traffic. This binding has so far contributed to poor punctuality. In practice, the separation of traffic can only be fully implemented from around 2022, when the electrification programme allows all the coastal railway trains to and from other stations on Zealand. At that time, the Coastal Railway will be able to avoid all Swedish influences. However, it cannot be assumed that all problems will disappear, for in the future delays from Zealand trains will have an impact on the coastline, but they typically have a better punctuality than the trains from Sweden have.
ParlaMint-DK_20170524145039	The questioner.

ParlaMint-DK_20170524152420	SF has made a proposal for a parliamentary decision to create a pool for local support for local integration houses. The comments on the motion for a resolution state: 'There is a greater risk that integration will not be achieved if you are outside the labour market, are poorly trained and do not acquire the Danish language. ▪ I just couldn't agree more so far, so well. So the question is, how do we solve this integration challenge? After all, the whole foundation of the Danish welfare society is the bourgeois service. It is a service for which the municipalities are responsible. The decentralized model that we have here in Denmark, where the municipalities have organized their efforts according to local conditions and needs, is quite unique, and it is in fact also my view that the model has broad political support. It is therefore the municipalities that are responsible for the integration efforts in Denmark. It is up to the municipalities to provide housing for Danish education and to support refugees in employment and self-supporting as soon as possible. So if we are to succeed with integration, it is absolutely essential that the municipal integration effort works. In March 2016, the government concluded an agreement with the National Association of Municipalities, which contains 57 concrete initiatives that provide municipalities with a better framework for receiving and integrating refugees. The initiatives will include: make it easier for municipalities to place refugees in housing, strengthen employment efforts and make Danish education more vocationally oriented. It is all to make it easier for municipalities to integrate refugees so that they can contribute and participate actively in Danish society as soon as possible. The trialogue agreement of March 2016 agreed on an attempt at basic integration training. Integration initial training is another tool that can help to get more refugees into employment. SF has put forward a proposal for the establishment of local integration houses according to the Kringlebakken model. Kringlebakken is an integration house located on Bispebjerg in the northwest district of Copenhagen, and Kringlebakken is a voluntary social association which has worked with integration and also empowerment of New Danish women and their children since 1999. When you read the comments on the SF's motion for a resolution, you can read that the integration houses must have many functions. That's why I'm just gonna do a couple of hits. It is clear that integration houses must act as a transitional period from home to education and the labour market for marginalised and isolated women and men with ethnic minority backgrounds. I simply do not understand what is meant by a transitional arrangement, because it is the municipality that is responsible for getting refugees and family members into employment. So what function will a transitional arrangement have in an integration house? If the transitional system becomes a waiting room for employment, I must admit that I actually find it very difficult to see the value of it, because the best way to break the isolation is to get a job. We know that business-oriented efforts work, so it is absolutely crucial that we get refugees and immigrants to work as soon as possible. And the solution is not, in the opinion of the government, an activation project in an integration house, but precisely to get unemployed women into business-oriented offers. With the integration allowance, the ceiling on cash benefits and the 225-hour rule, the government has ensured that work is worthwhile, and with the 225-hour rule, we give migrant women in particular a lever to get out of their homes and into the labour market. I would also like to point out here that the target group for integration houses is subject to the obligation to be available, as are all other beneficiaries of benefits in this country. It follows from SF's proposal that the integration houses should act as bridge builders for the municipal initiatives. In fact, I believe that this approach is completely flawed when it comes to the relationship between citizens and municipalities. I find it hard to see that it creates confidence if you tell a fugitive that there is a need for a bridge builder when you need to get in touch with the municipality. Then it may well be that there are some voluntary efforts that can complement and support the municipal effort. You have to be free to do that, and that is, of course, fine if that is the case. But it is the municipality that must have direct contact with the citizens. ParlaMint-DK_20170524154618	Thank you to the rapporteur of the Danish People's Party. Next in line, sir. Marcus Knuth as rapporteur for the Left.
ParlaMint-DK_20170524154637	Thank you, sir. President. I do not think it is a major secret that I believe that the integration efforts of the last few decades have been somewhat near a disaster, both because we saw red governments repeatedly relaxing where they should have tightened up, which led to too many who have come here, but also because many of those who have come here have placed themselves in a parallel society away from the labour market, away from participating in social Danish society. The question now is how to achieve the best integration for those who are in Denmark. For the Left, the cornerstone is the labour market. It is about making demands on people so that they enter the labour market and become part of society. It is in the labour market that you learn the Danish language, this is where you enter into social relations, and this is where you get the Danish culture and the Danish language under your skin. This is also where you learn social responsibility. And think about it from the kids' perspective. Is it not better for children to grow up with a father and a mother who goes to a workplace instead of sitting as part of the Danish transfer system? We need to get away from a pattern where we see people being lulled into sleep by welfare and inefficient integration programs and instead have a system where people actively participate in the Danish labour market and society. Furthermore, participation in the labour market will strengthen the competences of the refugees who have come here, and that is also a good thing, as I hope that as many as possible can return to their homeland when there is peace and thus have strengthened competences where they can help build up their homeland. You know, getting people to work is actually going really well. A recent study by the Danish Employers' Association shows that the employment of refugees and family members has more than doubled between 2015 and 2016. A major reason is to be found in the 2016 tripartite agreement, where the government, together with the social partners, put an increased focus on employment in the integration effort. In short, it is a paradigm shift from the fact that we were previously afraid to make demands on refugees, to the fact that we are now saying, yes, we want to make demands, refugees are going to work during the period they are in Denmark, even if they cannot Danish from day one. In addition, it is worth mentioning that we reduced the benefits for the new integration benefit, including the ceiling on cash benefits, and the figures speak for themselves. Thousands have entered work across internships, IGUs, wage subsidies and regular work. In relation to the specific resolution here, the question is whether it will then contribute through the so-called integration houses. Let me say that in the Left we are always pleased with proposals on how we can get more women of other ethnic origin into the labour market. So, of course, we are happy to hear suggestions. We agree that you are extremely disadvantaged if you are outside the labour market as a woman, if you are poorly educated, if you do not know the Danish language, as the resolution says. I think we all agree with that. But the question is whether this is the right path to integration, and we in the Left do not mean that. In other words, that unfunded integration houses are on top of all the measures that we have to get people into work, and in addition to some of the measures that the Minister mentioned before, is the way forward. We trust that the municipalities can carry out that task. We believe that integration houses, as was also mentioned by our colleague from the Radical Left before, will only increase bureaucracy and administrative costs. As I read the proposal, there is a built-in premise that municipalities should not, on their own, provide help to citizens in helping them to integrate, and that is a premise that we do not in any way buy. Let me make it clear that the Left believes that the responsibility lies with the municipalities, and in this respect let me highlight the government's two-part agreement with the National Association of Municipalities from March 2016. It contains 57 initiatives to help municipalities integrate refugees, and it shows that we in the Left are committed to creating the best possible conditions for the municipalities. In other words, a number of initiatives have already been put in place to get people into the labour market, and so we fear that this proposal could lead to more bureaucracy, and that is why we are opposed to the resolution. Thank you for the floor.

ParlaMint-DK_20170524162156	The debate is open. The Minister for Transport, Building and Housing.
ParlaMint-DK_20170524162221	SF and the Danish People's Party have submitted proposals for a parliamentary resolution on combating social dumping in the transport sector. In concrete terms, the motion for a resolution calls on the government to implement seven different initiatives in the area of freight transport. The seven initiatives concern to a large extent issues that the European Commission is planning to address with its proposals in the so-called road package, which is expected to be presented on 31 May 2017. Indeed, the road haulage sector is an area which is largely regulated at EU level. I think that is very important for us to keep in mind when discussing it. In Denmark, therefore, we usually strive not to make rules that are contrary to our EU legal obligations. That is why I think it is good that we now have the opportunity to negotiate a road package. Indeed, the aim of the road package is also to ensure the proper functioning of the internal market, which at the same time supports the fight against social dumping. I note that some of the initiatives in the motion for a resolution have been dealt with in the past and that they raise legal concerns. This applies to the initiative on tightening up the requirements for obtaining the right to cabotage in Denmark, where the definition of international transport proposed in the motion for a resolution was amended by the former Minister for Transport, Magnus Heunicke, after the Commission had initiated a infringement procedure against Denmark. This also applies to the initiative on registration in the RUT register of all foreign carriers, such as: has been dealt with by the Committee on cabotage and the Committee on the Prevention of Social Dumping. In this regard, the assessment was that the cabotage regulation completely eliminates the possibility of registering cabotage operations, and therefore an obligation to register in the RUT register is not possible. And this applies to the clamping of whole wagon trains, which we have also discussed in the Folketing in the past, where it was clear that the proposal is a matter of legal certainty and that the possibility of withholding the hauler makes it possible to recover the fines already today. Regarding the initiative on the introduction of permanent license plate scanners at the border and at selected transport hubs, it can be pointed out that the three government parties, together with the Danish People's Party and the Social Democratic Party as part of the 2017 Finance Act, agreed that the National Police, with contributions from the Road Directorate, should examine the possibility of establishing an experimental system using automatic license plate recognition as part of the control of illegal cabotage operations. As Minister for Transport, Building and Housing, I support this work, and I would like to see us focusing, on the basis of the experience gained by the National Police with the scheme, on how we can better enforce the rules in force so that we can limit the scope of illegal cabotage. Similarly, in the case of the initiative to introduce limited-term parking for a maximum of 25 hours, I am positive that we are looking at how to avoid resting sites becoming campsites for the disadvantage of concession holders and other users of resting places. Such a restriction would also allow the rule that drivers should not spend the regular weekly rest in the truck to be enforced. I do not think it would be appropriate to continue working on the motion for a resolution here in the Folketing. Instead, I would very much like us to take the fight in the European Union in relation to the Commission's road package in the areas to which the resolution relates, where competence has been transferred to the European Union. Here I will work to ensure that we have simpler and clearer rules that are easier to enforce. This also applies to the rules on combined transport, where I will work to ensure that the rules governing cabotage are applied to combined transport. In working with the road package, I hope for a close and good dialogue with the Danish Parliament so that we can achieve good results for the benefit of Danish companies and citizens. Against this background, I would like to reject the proposal.

ParlaMint-DK_20170524165728	Thank you for that, President. The Danish People's Party and the Socialist People's Party have, in a rare alliance, tabled this motion for a resolution to combat social dumping in the transport sector, and I would like to start by expressing my thanks for the proposal; it always demands respect when someone sits down and thinks about things and gives us the opportunity to have a sober and objective debate. I would say that I recognise the intentions of the proposal. That's good intentions. We must combat the concept of social dumping, but we must also dare to distinguish between what is legal cabotage and what is direct social dumping, namely illegal cabotage. I would also like to stress that the Left is also very concerned about the need for orderly conditions and the need for fair conditions of competition in road transport. Then I think I would also like to point out that there is perhaps a premise for the whole proposal, which is perhaps a little wrong. That is the point of suggesting that all driving by foreign lorries with foreign drivers should be social dumping. There I would like to refer to an answer that came under the previous government. We need to prevent our rest areas from being turned into campsites. It's untenable. I can't do that. That is why we need to find concrete solutions, and two main models could be that we are making a ban with a time limit, that is to say temporary parking, and then it is prohibited with a large amount of fines. Another model could be that after 24 hours you saw, for example. start to take a noticeable payment to keep with foreign trucks at such rest places. I think that will dampen it. In any case, I would like to ask the question: is it now the task of the state to provide rest area for parking for days? I do not think it is. In fact, it could be a task for private parking spaces on payment to solve this, instead of there being congestion in our rest areas. So it's been tested. It's like old wine in new bottles coming here. But the good news is that the road haulage package coming next week gives us the opportunity to discuss a completely new and hopefully much simpler definition of cabotage, and I look forward very much to discussing this across the parties, and hopefully we will also reach a broad political agreement on what we are going to fight for will be the result in Brussels in this area. Then there's the clamping thing. So it can't be done. The red parties have tried to do so, but this proposal still comes up. Overall, as I said, there are good intentions in this proposal. A number of the elements are good, and they are in the process of being introduced, and the Left is ready to negotiate to get the sensible things done here.

And Denmark is a small country where you can get very far when the border is as high as 150 km as the combined directive allows. I am thinking of the fact that, in view of the legal discussion on co-responsibility, proportionality and third-party liability that our proposal brought about and on which we in the Committee on Transport have had mini-consultations on several occasions, I would not recommend that the cargo be seized until the fine is paid. In fact, we believe that it is enough for the freight forwarder or the freight buyer to have the trouble of reloading into a rest stop, because it is true that the whole wagon train is being detained, but not the load inside the trailer. ParlaMint-DK_20170524175848	The debate is open. It is initiated by the Minister for Transport, Building and Housing.
ParlaMint-DK_20170524175908	B 123 concerns foreign purchases of rental properties in Denmark containing dwellings. The motion for a resolution calls for an explanation of the scope, the rules in force and the possibility of tightening up legislation. I would like to start by saying that the legislation to which the proposal relates does not fall within the area of the Housing Minister's resort. The rules on the right of foreign buyers to acquire Danish rental property containing dwellings are contained in the law on the acquisition of real estate, which is the responsibility of the Minister of Justice. Paragraph 1 of the Acquisition Act. 1 means that persons who have not resided permanently in Denmark for five years may acquire real estate only with the permission of the Minister of Justice. Similarly, the provision of Paragraph 1 (1) of the Acquisition Act entails: 2, that companies, associations etc., i.e. legal persons not established in Denmark, can also acquire real estate only with the permission of the Minister of Justice. For persons who have not resided permanently in Denmark for five years and for companies which have not registered their registered address in Denmark, it is therefore possible under the current rules to refuse authorisation for the acquisition of immovable property. On the basis of the acquisition law, Order No. 764 of 1995, which include: implies that companies from countries which are members of the EU or which have acceded to the EEA Agreement may acquire real estate rights in Denmark if the acquisition is a prerequisite for the pursuit of self-employed activities or the provision of services. According to Danish law as well as EU law, there is also wide scope for foreign nationals and companies to form companies under Danish law. Such companies, which are directly or indirectly controlled by foreign persons or companies but which are incorporated under Danish law and have their registered office in Denmark, will easily acquire real estate in Denmark. In addition to the opportunities available to EU and EEA companies, it will therefore generally be possible for foreign persons and companies to acquire rental property containing housing by the usual and lawful use of Danish companies. The provisions of the TFEU on freedom of movement, including the free movement of capital, limit the legislative measures that may be taken to regulate the right of foreign buyers to acquire Danish rental property. A possible tightening is therefore a matter of touching on the provisions of Union law on freedom of movement, including the free movement of capital, which is one of the four fundamental rights of Community law. The free movement of capital, together with the other fundamental rights, is a prerequisite for the internal market. Capital movements also include the exploitation, acquisition and disposal of real estate. It is therefore primarily the provisions on the free movement of capital which are relevant to the motion for a resolution, but it would also be appropriate to consider the other provisions of the TFEU on freedom of movement, for example. free movement of services and freedom of establishment. Under EU law, it is unlikely to be possible to introduce rules that discriminate directly between Danish and foreign buyers of rental property. It could then be considered whether other more indirect forms of discrimination could be introduced. However, any restriction on the right to purchase rental property in Denmark would constitute a restriction. Therefore, it becomes crucial whether such a restriction can be justified by the reasons set out in the Treaty establishing the EDF or by overriding reasons of the general interest and is to be regarded as proportionate. The motion for a resolution calls for foreign purchases to be the cause of rising prices in the real estate market, thus justifying intervention. If so, should that fact be examined further: is there any link at all between foreign purchases of rental property and higher prices on the rental property market, and will any restrictions constitute an overriding public interest and be suitable for the purpose in question and, finally, do not go beyond what is necessary? It is a burden of proof that I consider it absolutely impossible to lift. Firstly, the causal link goes the other way. It is not foreign investors that cause higher prices. It is the higher prices and expectations of future price increases that attract foreign investors. Secondly, price increases are not an isolated phenomenon in the rental property market. The whole housing market has increased dramatically, especially in large cities. I fully recognise the problem that lies at the root of this motion for a resolution, namely that housing prices have become unfairly high in the capital and in the larger cities. However, instead of spending time and effort examining measures which, on the one hand, cannot be implemented within EU law, and, on the other hand, would have no effect, even if they could be implemented, I would suggest that we look at the real causes of the high prices and what we can do about them. Price increases indicate that there is more demand for housing than there is supply. The answer to this is not increased restrictions on the housing market, but an increased supply of housing. We need to build more homes. It's the way to cheaper housing, and it's the only way. The extensive analyses that the resolution requires the government to carry out will be a waste of time and money. That is why the government cannot support the motion for a resolution. But the government wants to discuss the real question: how do we build more housing for cheaper money so that we can bring down prices on the housing market? I hope that the parties of the Folketing, including the proposers, will be open to discussion on this. ParlaMint-DK_20170524183602	The debate is open, and we shall give the floor first to the Minister for Transport, Building and Housing.
ParlaMint-DK_20170524183622	First of all, thank you to the Enhedslisten, the Social Democratic Party and the SF for the proposal. I would like to start by saying that the government does not support this. I would like to acknowledge that the Enhedslisten, the Social Democratic Party and the SF with the proposal touch on an issue on which the government is also focusing. The proposal concerns the prevention of forced entry into private homes and, in concrete terms, as paragraph 1, it is proposed that a proposal should be made to amend the building law so that adequate security can be required in the case of forced entry and as paragraph 2 it should be required in the building regulations that doors and windows should be burglar-proof so that they can keep burglars out for at least 3 minutes. Before embarking on a long and fundamental discussion about the right of homeowners to self-determination, I would like to start a completely different place with a quote from a discussion post published in Jyllands-Posten on 20 March 2017. Here, the director of the window industry, the manager of the Danish Construction Industry and the director of the trade in DI Wood and furniture industry writes the following about the proposal for compulsory burglary protection. Another consequence of the legal requirement will be that traditional Danish and especially wood-based window and door products will probably be illegal. This is because some very traditional Danish product types used for centuries cannot immediately meet EU standards for burglar-retardant products. In other words, with a requirement for burglar protection, you will be able to risk phasing out e.g. The formbrog windows, as you know, are one of the hallmarks of our proud building culture. Is that really what the proposers want? Will we build, as we do in Germany and England, with windows opening inwards, without more subjects and without the characteristic hasps and with frames of aluminum instead of wood? One can try to imagine the type of windows on a classic Danish masonry villa, of which there are many, and which is also a style that is popular for newly built houses. In addition, the discussion paper from Jyllands-Posten estimates that the proposed scheme will put 2,000-3,000 jobs at risk in the outermost regions of Denmark. This will particularly affect small and medium-sized enterprises which cannot afford to convert their production and develop evidence that their products meet the new requirements. We in the government do not think that is the way forward. If you then have to look a little more closely at the requirement for burglar protection, it is important to bear in mind that the number of break-ins is not the same across the country. There is therefore not the same need for special security everywhere. However, the proposed requirement for burglar protection will affect everyone, including those who live in a very low risk of forced entry. At the same time, it is not always enough to delay a burglar for 3 minutes if he or she remains undetected. The report from the Crime Prevention Council of 5 May 2017 on the Danish level of intrusion from an international perspective also points out that Denmark's many parcel houses surrounded by hedges provide very good conditions for the burglars, as the thieves are difficult to discover. For this type of housing it probably makes more sense to have alarm systems. For the citizen who does not see a need for special security or would rather secure his home by installing an alarm system, 5,000 kroner will. for burglar doors and windows be a significant additional cost, regardless of the cost of the building. In addition, the sum of DKK 5 000 mentioned in the proposal must be regarded as a minimum amount for traditional parcel houses, and a requirement for burglary protection can lead to even greater additional costs if you want to build on solutions other than the completely standardised ones. In addition, it must also be borne in mind that you can take out in-house insurance and that insurance companies have the possibility of carrying out a risk assessment of the property by pricing. The Government therefore considers that it must basically be up to the individual owner himself to assess the need for burglary protection, especially in view of the location and hence the break-in risk and the alternative security options available. As a consequence, the government considers that the owner of a traditional bricklayer's villa must be free to retain the formbrog window, if that is what is preferred. In addition, the requirement for burglary protection will impose considerable burdens not only on homeowners but also on business, and we cannot support that. Thank you for the floor.

ParlaMint-DK_20170524185142	No one else has asked for brief comments, so we thank the Minister. We move on to the rapporteur's line and the first rapporteur is Mrs Trine Bramsen, the Social Democratic Party.
ParlaMint-DK_20170524185157	The proposal here must ensure that we reduce the number of break-ins in the Danes' homes. We have listened to the experts, and we have looked at the research, which clearly and unequivocally points to the fact that shell safety is the best protection against burglary and thus it is what can ensure that we get the number of break-ins down in Denmark. Denmark has a European top ranking for breaking into private homes. It is even very few of the broken-ins that are solved. In fact, we are in a situation where the proportion of open-ended burglars is falling and the duration of proceedings for the investigation of burglars is increasing. That is why we need to think new and we need to look at both foreign experience and experts' assessment of what is working, because the current effort is not working well enough. All the experts are therefore pointing to the need to safeguard the shell, and that is the reason for the proposal that we are discussing here today. We want to amend the building law and regulations so that new buildings and major renovations require the use of larger window types which make it as difficult as ever for the thief to break in. Similar arrangements already exist in both the Netherlands and England, where this has proved to have a really great and beneficial effect. If you go to Holland and look at the houses there, you will also find that it is not the case that old houses suddenly look completely different. So just to address this with the tests of this type of windows, you know, those with the 3-minute protection, they're also the ones you use in the Netherlands, where the number of break-ins has been rattled down. So there's some evidence that it's working. In concrete terms, the proposal will mean that when you build a new house or make major renovations, there will be requirements for doors and windows. You rarely think about it yourself when you order a builder to build a new house or renovate a house, or when you traverse into Silvan to buy new windows. That is why it must be a legal requirement. This will mean that the market will quickly change • that is what they have seen in the Netherlands and England so that only doors and windows are offered that meet the standard. Three years ago, the former SR government launched a series of experiments at home, where the secured doors and windows were inserted into the houses in larger areas. The results of these experiments are perfectly clear: statistically speaking, there have been significantly fewer break-ins. We cannot simply ignore these results if, from a political point of view, we sincerely want to reduce the number of break-ins in Denmark. On the contrary, we should extend the system to the rest of the country. May I also point out at the same time that our police are under historical pressure and will probably be so many years ahead. That is why we need to take action with the preventive initiatives that work. To those parties who are in favour of prevention but who do not support the proposal here, I have to say quietly that I do not understand the reasoning. Because if you've tried breaking and entering, you know how insulting and insecure it is. It is our duty, as politicians, to create a secure society without concern for break-in. And if we now know that there are solid and well-tested tools that work, then we must listen to it, and we must use the tools. Everything else is a failure. Critics will claim, and so did the Minister, that it is an expensive element when you build new or remodel. The truth is, of course, and it has also been extensively investigated that it is a minimal cost and that the minimum additional cost will be quickly offset by the costs that would have been if you had a break-in and that is not least socio-economic. Let us now seriously crack the burglary basket in Denmark, let us not be satisfied that Danish citizens are among those in Europe who most often experience coming home, where there are important possessions and historical memories that have been removed and where there is insecurity in the future. Let's really do it, it works. In those words, I would like to say that we support the proposal.
ParlaMint-DK_20170524185623	No one has asked for brief comments, so we go further in the rapporteur's line to Mr Beazley. Peter Kofod Poulsen, Danish People's Party.
ParlaMint-DK_20170524185637	Thank you. With the resolution we are now considering, the Social Democracy, the SF and the Enhedslist will make a change to the building regulations, which will require manufacturers to be able to withstand a burglar for three minutes with their windows and doors in connection with new buildings and major renovations. I would like to start by saying that the Danish People's Party cannot support the resolution. The argument in the proposal is based largely on the idea that windows and doors should withstand the thief for 3 minutes, and I think that one would be able to look at the test used and assess whether one thinks that the tools a thief should have available in the test also correspond to the tools that a thief in the real world will use. I can only see that the test that assesses the resistance in the doors and in the windows can be questioned very seriously. I therefore do not believe in the premise that something should be burglar-proof for 3 minutes only if it meets the test that has been carried out here. If something really is to be burglar-proof in the real world, it will require far more than what is being demanded today, and it will therefore be much more expensive than the starting point which suggests that the cost will be about DKK 5 000. for a newly built parcel house, and that would be a conservative estimate. It is precisely the fact that this is costly and difficult for producers that is also an essential part of the rejection of the resolution. By introducing this requirement, we risk exposing an already tough competitive sector to even more pressure from foreign competition. We are simply at risk of costing Danish jobs and of noticing some of the places where it has to be said that it is very sluggish to set private employment in motion, and we find that very difficult. But if we stick for a moment to the Danish break-ins, there is no doubt that there is everything, too many break-ins in Denmark, and that is a huge challenge. If we look at the basket and take a historical look at the figures, it is clear that the number of break-ins from the mid-1980s until the mid-00's is relatively stable at between 30,000 and 35,000 break-ins in private homes annually. In 2008 there is a serious explosion in the number, which rises to over 40,000, and there you are for a number of years until the figure starts to stabilise again on what has been the normal number. As far as I am concerned, there is no doubt that the sudden increase has one single reason: a series of countries were admitted into Schengen in 2008. In other words, much of this is about border controls, border controls and border controls. At least that's what we can point to as the clear trend based on the numbers we have. We can do a lot of things. One reason why there are so many break-ins in Denmark is that the clearing rate is far too low. Quite a few percent of the break-ins are solved, and that really means that the burglars can continue and continue without being held to account. So we're missing a plug in the hole. In other words, there needs to be a marked slowing down here, which is why it is essential that the administrative effort is looked at in the seams. We can look at the punishment for breaking and entering. I think it would be important to send a very clear signal to people who are considering breaking and entering, that we are being punished for committing this kind of crime. Then something has to be done about the fence, because there is an excessive market in Denmark, where it is too easy for heelers to sell and sell large quantities of stolen goods, and we must take action against that too. In addition, I believe that we should strengthen controls, particularly border checks at Danish borders, in order to stop thieves and fences from bringing these stolen goods to other countries. I think that should be a matter of course when we look at the history and the Danish figures. I look forward to discussing a new burglary package during the summer and autumn, which will help to curb the number of break-ins. There are too many break-ins, and that is why we need to look at many points, not just the point that has been put forward here today, but looking at the whole area and providing it with a service. ParlaMint-DK_20170524193515	Thank you for the floor, President. There are approx. 32,000 break-ins in Denmark a year. There are 6.5 per cent who are being solved, which means that 93.5% are not being solved. We know from studies, we know from research, we know from experts that there is a means that is particularly effective in preventing burglary, and that is a better shell protection. Often, when we talk about crime prevention, and many times, when I have stood on this platform as Legal Rapporteur for the Enhedslisten, it has been about the need for higher penalties, more punishment with punishment, even though in virtually every case there is no proven preventive effect. Here we have an area where we can actually go in and do something about prevention, which we know works, which will have a very small additional cost and which will have an impact on thousands of people in this country. And we know from the key players in the field, TrygFonden, Insurance & Pension, the Crime Prevention Council and the SecurityBranch that this is the way to approach the matter. We have seen experiences from other countries, from the Netherlands and England, where there are legal requirements for burglary protection for new buildings and larger conversions, and where there must be a minimum penetration time of 3 minutes. And in both countries we have seen a halving in the number of break-ins in newly built homes. After all, these are experiences which are concrete experiences and which we can also use to make changes to legislation at home. We have also seen that the price of making this improved shell protection with a minimum penetration time of 3 minutes will be very small. In the report prepared by COWI for the Danish Energy Agency in July 2015, we can see that the consumer price for the proposed minimum penetration time of 3 minutes is 5,000 DKK. for a newly built parcel house of 150 m2 with a construction cost of 2.25 million. kr., i.e. 5,000 kr. in relation to 2.25 million. kr. It has to be said that this is a price increase which is trivial compared with the other costs of construction. And in this respect, it should also be borne in mind that, if this is to become standard at some point, it will happen that it will go from being a niche product to becoming a standard product, and that will reduce the price even further. It is also interesting that there is a discussion in many other European countries about this, and when you look at how many break-ins there are actually in Denmark, you can ask yourself whether Denmark really should be the last country to implement improved shell protection. Does the fact that we have lax legislation and a lax building regulations in this area help us to get even more break-ins in Denmark compared to what the situation looks like in other countries • when we now have the very boring second place we have? Because we are the country in Europe that has the second most burglaries per year. citizens, only surpassed by Greece. It's not a record that anyone in this thing is proud of. I just think we should do that. But I really think that the debate has been very good in this way, because if it is possible to find a common basis here, which will have a clear crime prevention effect, without us constantly having to talk punishment with punishment, then there is a basis for further work, and I think it would be good if the opposition could find a sensible and pragmatic solution here. Then we also have the possibility that if we can agree on a sensible and pragmatic basis, hopefully it will not be long before we have a new majority and a new government. Then we can sit down, and then we don't just have to write things down on a piece of paper. Then, in fact, we can make something that I hope is sensible, a concrete reality for the benefit of thousands of people in this country, low crime prevention; do something that fights crime; have a sensible approach to crime prevention, which is not always just about symbolic policy and punishment, but which is about preventing crime in a sensible way in the real world.

ParlaMint-DK_20170524194144	Thank you. Since 1 May 2004, the Danish labour market has been open to Eastern European labour force and it has created a large number of problems which quickly got the common term social dumping. It includes both underpayment, cheating with overtime and other conditions normally regulated in agreements, and it also includes breaches of legislation, for example. non-payment of holiday money, poor working environment or non-payment to SKAT. Of course, the unfairly low wages must be combated. There must be Danish pay in Denmark. First of all, it is up to our trade unions and employers' organisations to deal with it. However, the extensive breaches of Danish legislation, which cover both ignorance and outright cheating, are of course the responsibility of the authorities, while here in the Danish Parliament we have a responsibility that both the social partners and the authorities are supported in their work to ensure equal conditions and competition for Danish jobs. From 2004 to 2011, the Danish Parliament took not so many initiatives to meet this responsibility, but on the Finance Acts from 2012 until today, money has been set aside for the police, Labour Inspectorate, SKAT and the Danish Agency for International Recruitment and Integration in cooperation to strengthen control of foreign companies and employees in Denmark. That effort has now worked for five years and, in our view, has been constantly improving. We now want to use this five-year anniversary as an opportunity to stop and discuss the joint efforts of the authorities in order to continue and strengthen it. Of course, we thank the government for showing up and looking forward to today's debate.
ParlaMint-DK_20170524194320	Thank you. Then it is my pleasure to welcome the Minister for Employment himself for an answer. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20170524194327	Thank you for that and also thank you for the opportunity for this joint debate on the joint action against social dumping by a large number of parties. This is an effort which the majority of the parties here in the Folketing have helped to support through a wide range of different financial agreements concluded both under the present but also under previous governments, and as Mr. Mattias Tesfaye also mentioned in his explanatory statement, it is an effort that has stretched over a number of years. The work we are taking stock of today has been going on for five years, and it is therefore also very relevant that we in the Parliamentary Assembly should have a discussion on the experience to date and, above all, a political debate on the desires and visions for future action. Let me also mention in this connection that there has also been a technical review for a number of parliamentary committees, and that I myself, together with the tax minister and a number of officials from the Ministry of Taxation, the Ministry of Employment and the Ministry of Justice, have made myself available here a few weeks ago to take stock. It is a key priority for the government that we have effective action that helps to ensure orderly conditions on the Danish labour market, including helping to combat what is defined as social dumping. This is also very clear from the government basis. Foreign companies and employees are welcome in Denmark, but it is out of the question that they should comply with our labour market rules and that foreign employees should work under the pay and working conditions that apply here in Denmark. As politicians, we are responsible for ensuring a sensible framework for the work of the authorities and for the Danish labour market, and the social partners are responsible for ensuring pay and working conditions. It provides a well-functioning, but also flexible labour market. Therefore, the government gives priority to foreign companies having to comply with the Danish rules so that they do not win at unfair competition. In other words, we must maintain a functioning and flexible labour market here in Denmark. That is why I am also pleased that, most recently with the 2017 Finance Act, we have retained the appropriation for the Joint Authority's efforts to combat social dumping in 2017 and 2018. When it comes to securing orderly conditions, including combating social dumping, we focus on several areas of action. Let me start with the authority effort. We have maintained the authority's efforts, in which the Labour Inspectorate, SKAT and the police are concerned with joint and coordinated control actions, because joint action has proved to be an effective tool for getting hold of companies that do not comply with the rules. Since 2012, funds have been allocated to the strengthened efforts to combat social dumping, after which control and supervision of the rule of conduct of foreign companies has increased significantly. The authorities shall carry out joint control operations and shall have day-to-day activities in this area. In 2016 and 2017, the focus has continued to be on improving the effectiveness of controls, so that resources are used where the impact of the authorities' efforts is greatest. It's kind of common sense. In addition, the authorities have also worked to obtain new knowledge and develop tools so that efforts remain timely. Finally, the authorities focus on new trends among foreign companies doing work here in Denmark. In order to support this work, the authorities are in continuous dialogue on both industry and enterprise selection for control efforts, precisely in order to ensure that the most risky industries and businesses are given priority. I can inform you that 38 national joint actions and 131 regional joint actions have been carried out in the period 2012-2016. During the period 2012-2016, the Danish Labour Inspectorate has visited 11.517 foreign companies and submitted 6,348 reactions. Nearly three quarters of the reactions concerned serious violations of the law, such as bans or immediate injunctions. In the regulatory work, SKAT helps to ensure compliance with the rules in the field of taxation. And I have been informed that in the period 2015-2016 SKAT has carried out 7,136 checks, of which 2,394 controls have ended up in regulation, and the calculated net revenue is 413 million. kr. during the period. The police provide assistance for the administrative control work of the Tax and Labour Inspectorate and carry out checks on the residence and working conditions of foreigners and investigate suspected criminal offences. I have been informed that the police's experience in cases of illegal work in 2016 includes: has resulted in 463 charges against workers for illegal work and 336 charges against employers for illegal work. In addition, cooperation means that the Labour Inspectorate, the police and the SKAT will continue to be even better at supporting each other's exercise of authority and ensuring effective control. So in other words, the joint action by the authorities is absolutely central and, fortunately, also has broad political support here in the Folketing and, for that matter, also clear support from the social partners. Let me also mention bilateral cooperation. Indeed, we have strengthened cooperation with the authorities in other countries where we can and must do more to ensure that more foreign companies in Denmark comply with the rules. The Ministry of Employment has concluded bilateral cooperation agreements with four Eastern European countries, Poland, Lithuania, Latvia and Slovakia, to strengthen cooperation between authorities in the field of secondment. I attach great importance to better knowledge of the labour market models of the various countries by the authorities and to better cooperation in the exchange of information and information. This can help to ensure greater transparency, greater transparency, including for companies and employees who work across borders. Let me also make a few comments on the RUT register. The establishment of the RUT register in 2008 is a joint project based on a broad political agreement to provide the authorities and the social partners with information about foreign companies here in Denmark. We have good experience with RUT registered, which in many ways is a focal point for the common authority's efforts. The RUT register has proved to be an extremely effective tool to support the authorities and the social partners in their respective tasks of ensuring orderly relations with foreign companies. These are the kinds of tools that I think we benefit most from. The effort shows, for example, that foreign companies notified in RUT are more in line with health and safety legislation than foreign companies that do not comply with the RUT reporting obligation. On average, 57% is paid. of the foreign companies which are not in the RUT register, a serious occupational health and safety reaction. The corresponding figure for the companies in the RUT is approx. 1.7% So this is a marked and noticeable difference. Therefore, we will also maintain and develop the RUT register as a focal point for both the authorities and the social partners in ensuring orderly conditions. Last year, we have also adopted two legislative proposals implementing the enforcement directive, which support the work on orderly matters. This means, inter alia, that the Labour Inspectorate has been given the opportunity to identify mailbox companies that abuse the posting rules. At the same time, RUT will be expanded, which will now contain information that will help to clarify whether companies have real activities in their home country. Let me also make a few comments on the whole discussion that has been about the increased focus on illegal labour. The government looks very seriously at the pockets of the Danish labour market where you can get away with employing third-country nationals without legal residence and without work permits. We need to focus much more on this in the future, because we simply cannot accept it. That is why the government has also recently set up a working group to look at the experience of the past efforts and, above all, the possibilities of making enhanced efforts. The Working Party has the participation of the Ministry of Foreign Affairs and Integration, the Ministry of Justice, the Ministry of Taxation and the Ministry of Employment and expects to be able to complete its work later this year. I can inform you that, for a period of time, the Labour Inspectorate will list the times when information is passed on to the police on the basis of suspicions of illegal labour. In this way we will learn more about the scale of the problem. In addition, the Minister for Foreign Affairs and Integration has indicated that it would look at the possibility of tightening the penalties against employers who are using illegal labour. At the same time, we need to create much greater transparency and transparency about the Danish labour market, so that foreign companies and employees have better knowledge and easier access to information about what is applicable here in Denmark. But let me also stress here today that this is a debate which I also look forward to having with the parties of the Folketing. In the same way, it can be said that the European Union, which is in many ways an institution that ensures freedom of movement and is therefore also the cause of some of the problems we see, presents a dilemma. However, since we are at least of the opinion that the free movement of labour and capital is one of the essential prerequisites for our prosperity and wealth in this country, we also believe that we must maintain these mechanisms with the disadvantages that may exist, and I actually believe, and I said this in my first speech here earlier today, that it is within the framework of the European Union that many of these solutions must be found. Particularly in the area of transport, for which we spent a great deal of time, it is quite evident that the issues we are discussing are cross-border, and then it must also be a matter of getting common definitions and a common understanding of what these are for some sizes we have to deal with legally. I think that when we deal with these problems of social dumping, we should also bear in mind that we cannot do without foreign labour; sometimes we get a feeling that it is really bad that foreigners can even come to Denmark and work; that is what is also part of the dilemma when we have a large market and we have very different conditions. We have to deal with that, and I think we can do it too. I imagine that the fact that we are digitising our society more and more will help in solving the problems that we are seeing. After all, we are getting more transparency, and we also talked about that in the transport sector. I would like to conclude by saying that the Minister has, of course, explained the efforts that have been made and how effective they have really been, and I think that is actually a great many things we are getting for 70-80 million. kr., or how much it is, when you consider that it is in the thousands of interventions to be made, also in relation to the SKAT. So we must continue to do so, and this is also clearly stated in the text. So I would say: remember, it is not always the number of crowns, it depends, it is also about how we organise the effort and how we use the technology! So we can actually improve our efforts without necessarily pulling more crowns out of taxpayers' money.
